@@1493229 <h> Overseas aid is being spared as Britain faces the biggest cuts in its history , writes Andrew Gilligan . <p> With less than a month to go before the biggest-ever spending cut in the history of Britain , the anger it is sure to provoke will be increased by the fact that the wrong programmes are going to be protected and the wrong people are going to be written cheques . <p> If we are to shield any spending from the axe , it should surely be that which improves our chances of economic recovery , and stimulates future tax revenues . But no . Forget education or job-creating investment . Only two budgets are sacred : the NHS , and overseas aid . <p> Let 's concentrate on the latter . It is surely a stupid proposition that social services , schools , housing and the rest should take higher-than-necessary cuts in order to protect the untouchable empire of the Department for International Development ( DFID ) . <p> I do not believe that charity should begin at home . I believe that the plight of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that the kind of charity doled out by the overseas aid budget is often fundamentally ill-directed , or worse . <p> Looking at the figures I discover that , over the last five years for which data are available , Britain has given ? 8.7 million in development aid to Singapore , whose gross domestic product per capita is the fourth highest in the world , and 46 per cent higher than our own . <p> Over the same period , other recipients of British development aid have included Slovenia , Malta , the Czech Republic and Hungary , all highly developed First World countries , fellow members of the EU , and two of whom are even in the euro . In the last year for which figures are available , Britain also gave ? 380,000 in aid to the enormously wealthy oil sheikhdom of Saudi Arabia . <p> These are , of course , relatively small sums . Yet DFID also paid ? 40.2 million last year to the economic superpower that is China , now officially classified by the World Bank as a " middle-income country " . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ encourage Chinese children to campaign against climate change . <p> The China aid programme is scheduled to end in 2011 . But Britain 's commitment to that other Asian titan , India , shows no sign of letting up . Over the past 10 years , even as India 's economy has roared into overdrive , British development aid to the country has almost trebled . <p> In the last year for which full figures were available , 2007/8 , it was ? 312 million ( ? 275 million of it from DFID ) -- making India , by a very long way , the largest single recipient of British aid . Can it be right that the British taxpayer gives such sums to a nation which can afford its own nuclear weapons ? <p> Of course , India has hundreds of millions of poor people . But it is now so prosperous that it has started a significant overseas aid programme of its own . In the words of MPs on the International Development Select Committee , India also " seems to have become increasingly tired of being cast in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and declining all which is conditional on things such as government reform . <p> The Indian government refuses to allow DFID to work directly with most local charities , insisting that payments be processed through official channels . This , said the MPs , caused a " lack of leverage , difficulties in tracking money trails , and problems in determining outcomes " . <p> The majority of DFID 's spending -- albeit , at 56 per cent , a rather narrower majority than you 'd expect -- does go to what are known as the " least developed countries " , mostly in Africa . Even here , though , there is a growing consensus that decades of international aid have often actually made things worse . Moses Isooba , of Uganda 's Community Development Resource Network , says that " a majority of civil society actors in Africa see aid as a fundamental cause of Africa 's deepening poverty " . Some of the reasons are fairly clear . Poverty 's real parent is not cruel Nature , but bad government . Even perhaps the most famous humanitarian crisis @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ by a civil war , rather than drought or crop failures . But aid has often worked against good government , by reducing the need for local politicians to take much notice of their own people . <p> Less obviously , massive international-aid flows can have hugely destabilising macroeconomic effects , dramatically changing the value of local currencies and affecting prices and incentives . <p> Of course , aid is complicated . Of course , it sometimes helps . DFID does understand the issues , and one of its key priorities is governance . But it is extremely noticeable how , even as well-meaning Whitehall liberals have poured in the aid , Africans themselves have started to reject it . As the analyst Jonathan Glennie puts it , " it is hard to find a single example of an African NGO that is actively campaigning for aid increases " . <p> The unfortunate truth is that , like those Bob Geldof pop concerts , an awful lot of aid spending is actually for our benefit , not the Third World 's . All too often , its main effect is not @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ feel good about themselves . <p> The aid budget is a totem of our niceness -- which is presumably why the Cameron Tory party , ever alert to a symbolic gesture , has decided to protect it . But it 's a totem which needs just as much cold , hard examination as any other part of the Government budget . 
@@1493316 <p> At the world 's biggest gathering of climate change sceptics , organised by the right-wing Heartland Institute , vegetarians were an endangered species . <p> Wine flowed and blood coursed during a rousing address from Heartland 's libertarian president Joseph Bast . Mr McIntyre urged the audience to support the battle for open source data on climate change - but then he counselled them to stop clamouring for the blood of the e-mailers . All that was needed was an acknowledgement by the science authorities that the Hockey Stick was wrong . <p> Political associations <p> This was clearly not the sort of emollient message the sceptics expected from one of their heavy hitters . @@1493317 <h> David Cameron defended the coalition Government 's decision to defer decisions on many issues where the Tories and Liberal Democrats have been unable to agree . <p> The Prime Minister insisted that the two parties agreed on most policies , even as he unveiled an agreement that put off many decisions by establishing independent reviews and commissions . <p> The coalition has not clearly defined policy on more than 20 issues , promising instead to take a position at some point . <p> Mr Cameron and his Lib Dem deputy , Nick Clegg , hailed the agreement as a historic act of " partnership " . The Prime Minister told reporters it was " churlish " to focus on the number of issues where the coalition had deferred decisions . <p> " It 's the shortage of commissions rather than the amount of them that Her Majesty 's press corps should be focusing on , " he said . " There are so many commitments that are solid , bankable , deliverable . " <p> There will be commissions on issues including whether to split banks ' @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to Scotland ; and how to fund care for the elderly . <p> The Tories promised to scrap the Human Rights Act , a law that the Lib Dems have defended . The coalition text confirmed that the issue would be reviewed , as would Tory plans to assert the supremacy of Parliament over the European Union . <p> Other issues put under review included : local government finance ; public sector pensions ; reform of the House of Lords ; rights to flexible working ; control orders for terrorist suspects ; and sentencing in English courts . <p> Before the publication of yesterday 's document , some Tories were unhappy at the initial compromises Mr Cameron had made to secure his coalition with Mr Clegg 's party . <p> The Prime Minister accepted that some members of his party would be unhappy about certain details of the deal , but he insisted that it would be welcomed overall because it would deliver " strong and stable government " . <p> " Of course , people will be disappointed that some policies have had to be discarded , " he said @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ it 's about a shared vision . " <p> The final coalition text , The Coalition : our programme for government , confirmed that another of the Tories ' tax-cutting promises had been sacrificed to secure the Lib Dem deal . <p> The manifesto promise to cut stamp duty for first-time buyers has been dropped for an independent review of thresholds . <p> Mr Cameron shifted his position on several other issues important to Right-wing Tories , including shelving promises to cut inheritance tax and give tax breaks to married couples , and proposing increases in capital gains tax . <p> What follows is a summary of the 31 policies covered by the 32-page document . <p> Banking <p> Britain 's big banks will be broken up as part of an overhaul of the sector to avoid a repeat of the financial crisis . But no action is expected immediately as a review looking at separating retail and investment banking will take at least a year . <p> Action will be taken against " unacceptable " bonuses and banks face new lending targets . A banking levy will be introduced but @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> Business <p> Red tape will be cut , with a " one-in one-out " rule under which no new regulation is brought in without another regulation being axed . <p> Corporation tax will be reformed by simplifying reliefs and allowances , with the aim of making Britain the most competitive regime in the G20 . The Government also aims to encourage new start-ups by reducing the number of forms needed to register a new business . <p> Civil Liberties <p> Human freedoms , which have been " abused and eroded " , will be restored " in keeping with Britain 's tradition of freedom and fairness " . <p> In particular , the Government will introduce a Freedom Bill , scrap the ID cards scheme , halt new biometric passports , review libel laws to protect freedom of speech , and regulate the widespread use of CCTV . <p> There will be a commission on a Bill of Rights to " build on " the European Convention on Human Rights and promote " understanding of the true scope of these obligations and liberties " . <p> Communities and Local @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ power from Westminster to people " . Local councils , communities and neighbourhood schemes will be given more power to determine local issues by a " radical devolution of power " . <p> Local councils will be given powers to stop " garden grabbing " by developers and to bring empty homes back into use . Council tax in England will be frozen for at least a year , and possibly two years ; the 12 largest English cities will be given directly-elected mayors ; councils will be banned from using anti-terrorism laws to snoop on people . <p> Consumer Protection <p> A limit will be put on the interest rates that companies can charge customers on bank and store cards . Any card charging more than 25 per cent could be banned . <p> However , financial experts said the move , while welcome , would be meaningless if the Government did not tackle the wider problem of high debt charges elsewhere . The coalition document did not , for instance , mention either " door step lending " or " payday loans " , a type of borrowing in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 2,000 per cent for short-term loans . <p> The Government has also promised to look again at the issue of " unfair " bank charges , although there was no suggestion that the millions already affected would be refunded . <p> Other measures included clearer food labelling , specifically a suggestion that supermarkets be forced to detail the origin of any meat in a ready meals . <p> Gas bills will be made clearer for customers by providing information on how to move to the cheapest tariff offered by their supplier , and how each household 's energy usage compares to similar households . <p> Crime and Policing <p> New measures to tackle alcohol-fuelled disorder form a key part of the crime and policing pledges . <p> There will be a ban on retailers selling alcohol at below cost price amid concerns that promotions have fuelled the binge-drinking culture . <p> Theresa May , the Home Secretary , has already promised to review 24-hour licensing , introduced in 2005 , but there will also be a review of alcohol taxation and pricing . <p> The maximum fine for selling alcohol to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ will be given the power to charge more for late-night licences to help fund the necessary additional policing that is required . <p> Under drug enforcement , any new substance marketed as a legal recreational drug will be prohibited instantly to allow a full assessment by the Advisory Council on the Misuse of Drugs . <p> The public will be better protected in law if they confront criminals , and police authorities will be replaced with directly-elected individuals . <p> Culture , Olympics , Media and Sport <p> The Government faces a battle with the BBC over spending , after confirming that it plans to give the National Audit Office full access to the corporation 's accounts . That will spell the end of Labour 's proposals for government-funded regional news programmes , which were intended to replace ITV 's loss-making regional news services . <p> The Government said it would " urgently " move forward with plans for a " genuine and lasting " legacy for the 2012 London Olympics . <p> Defence <p> An ambitious cut of " at least 25 per cent " in Ministry of Defence running costs is promised by the Government , amid controversy over the levels of bureaucracy in the department . <p> At the same time the coalition is promising to double the operational allowance for Armed Forces personnel serving in Afghanistan , and include Armed Forces pay in its plans for a fair pay review . <p> The document highlights a potential coalition split on the issue of renewing the Trident @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ project , but the Lib Dems say it is too expensive . " <p> There is a promise of university scholarships for the children of those killed on active duty since 1990 and a new programme called Troops for Teachers to recruit ex-Service personnel into the teaching profession . <p> Deficit reduction <p> Promising immediate action to tackle the deficit in a " fair and responsible way " , the document says the " main burden " will be borne by reduced spending rather than tax rises . <p> People on low incomes will be protected from public sector pay squeezes . @@1493422 <h> Rebuilding Britain : marching into a confident future <h> In the final article of our series on reshaping the nation , Charles Powell explains how we must adapt to an ever-changing world . <p> British soldiers march in Red Square in Moscow to mark the 65th anniversary of the defeat of Nazi Germany <p> 3:37PM BST 21 May 2010 <p> Labour has left a structural deficit in national security . <p> Muddled thinking about Britain 's role led to a mismatch between what we ask of our Armed Forces and diplomats and the resources we give them . It 's not , however , an East of Suez moment when Britain has to make an enormous down-shift in its world role . To fix the national security deficit we need to rationalise , to be more selective , and to spend limited resources more effectively . <p> The starting point for determining our role has to be the world as it will be , not as it was . It 's going to be more dangerous , more competitive , and more governed by great powers acting @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ threat to us may be less than at the height of the Cold War , but the dangers we do face , principally proliferation of weapons of mass destruction and terrorism , are more likely actually to happen . <p> * We shall have to focus relentlessly on making our businesses more competitive , bench-marking ourselves in future against Shanghai and Bangalore , not Paris and Berlin . <p> * The world system is going to re-nationalise . Multilateral institutions will be there as enablers . But the decisions will be in the hands of the major powers , such as Brazil , Russia , India and China , as well as the US , as we saw at the Copenhagen climate change summit . It will feel more like the 19th century than the late 20th century . <p> A weak economy leads to quick fixes <p> The first step , as in any rebuilding , is to make the foundations safe and strong . Restoring the UK economy is the most pressing national security task . A weak and debt-ridden economy hobbles us from playing the role in the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ for quick fixes and short-term solutions rather than a consistent long-term strategy . Margaret Thatcher demonstrated after 1979 how re-invigorating the British economy restores national self-confidence and earns international respect . <p> The second step is a long overdue strategic defence review , which puts our foreign and defence policies on a new and more rational footing . Plenty of new thinking will be required : <p> * The new Government has made a good start by setting up a national security council to draw together all the strands and make sure resources are distributed not according to departmental wish-lists but in support of cross-departmental policies . In particular , our lady bountiful Department for International Development needs to be re-nationalised . It can no longer behave as a taxpayer-funded international NGO , rather than as part of the government . Its programmes must be deployed much more in line with our national interests and priorities . <p> * The Ministry of Defence will have to stop thinking in terms of trade-offs between the single services when it comes to ordering new weapons and equipment so that everyone gets something . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the sort of conflict they will be expected to fight , matched by an assurance of greater discipline among our politicians , which will stop us embarking on missions that stretch resources and forces unacceptably . Where economies in the defence budget have to be made -- and they will -- they should be done rationally . <p> * The Foreign Office 's budget has been absurdly skimped in recent years and its talents directed to politically correct tasks more than national interests . It will need to shift from its love affair with multilateral institutions and rediscover its historic skills at bilateral relations and forming shifting alliances or coalitions of the willing . <p> * The European Union will remain an important theatre of our diplomacy but it is too cumbersome and risk averse to be an adequate substitute for our national role . We should regard it primarily as a burden-sharing vehicle , a market and a means of leverage in international trade . It must not be a drag on our ability to compete or to take our own decisions in defence of our global interests . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ us , then who ? ' <p> There is always a temptation to ask , why should Britain take on the burdens and costs of playing a prominent and active role in world affairs ? Why not be just another unambitious , mid-ranking European country ? Fortunately , it is in the British DNA to know that that is not an option for our national safety and our hopes of remaining a prosperous country . <p> The real question is not " why us ? " but " if not us , then who " ? The US can not be expected to bear the burden alone . Most other allies simply do n't have the political will to tackle the big security issues . Unless the UK contributes to common causes , America will become progressively less respectful of our interests . <p> In short , Britain should have the self-confidence that as a nation we are better placed than most to make the right choices and do the right thing . And the sooner the Government gets over its obsession about not looking slavish towards our most important ally @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , former diplomat and was foreign policy adviser to Margaret Thatcher 
@@1493446 <h> ' Playing God ' with the climate ? <p> One of the potential uses which he 's looking at for synthetic bacteria - sucking carbon dioxide from the atmosphere - potentially also breaks new ground in the ethics of human effects on the natural world . <p> Dr Venter 's proposed CO2-suckers , if they ever materialise , would basically constitute a new entry into the field of geo-engineering - using technology to ameliorate human-induced climate change . <p> Existing proposals include those with a notion of biology , such as using iron filings to stimulate the growth of oceanic plants or switching to growing crops with reflective leaves . <p> But most lie firmly in the physical domain - whitening roofs , putting a giant sunshade in space , or spraying the atmosphere with dust to mimic the sun-reflecting and planet-cooling effect of volcanic eruptions . <p> If you tend towards apocalyptic thoughts , here 's one for you : out-of-control CO2-sucking bacteria that multiply beyond measure , hoovering up every last molecule of the stuff and leaving none for plants and trees . <p> OK @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , I know - but even if your thoughts do n't run in a B-movie direction , you might conclude that here is a technology that would require a great deal of contained research and soul-searching and international agreement before it was ever let loose on the natural world . <p> So far , the international community of nations has been lagging behind the entrepreneurism of start-up geo-engineering visionaries . <p> Ocean fertilisation was first out of the blocks , with universities and research institutions mounting so far a dozen or so field investigations over the last decade , with mixed results - and with at least two companies , Climos and Planktos , aiming to use the approach commercially . <p> A couple of weeks ago , The Times reported that Bill Gates was funding a project co-ordinated by the San Francisco-based Silver Lining Project that would trial cloud-building - sucking up water from the sea and spraying it into the air , providing nuclei for the formation of clouds that would reflect solar energy back into space . <p> As Ben Webster noted in his article : <p> " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ wait for international rules on technology that deliberately alters the climate . <p> " They believe that the weak outcome of December 's climate summit in Copenhagen means that emissions will continue to rise unchecked and that the world urgently needs an alternative strategy to protect itself from global warming . " <p> Russia , too , is seeing the beginnings of real-life geo-engineering experiments . <p> I 'm told that this summer , the Russian group may try larger experiments that involve ejecting the dust from the back of a plane . <p> Why senior Russian advisers should be interested in this technology is n't immediately obvious , given that Russia is more lukewarm than most other countries to the idea of curbing global warming , and given that ex-President Vladimir Putin has spoken of the benefits that a bit of warming could bring to his nation . <p> Irrespective of the whys and wherefores though , the point is that geo-engineering research is happening , here and now . <p> So far it 's been on a fairly small scale . But at some point , if you 're @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ effect , you have to move out into the big field - and if you take the IPCC 's projections seriously , you 'd need to start doing it soon . <p> How any such large-scale research projects should be evaluated , regulated and monitored - and who should control such undertakings - is an issue that 's slowly emerging into the daylight . <p> The American Enterprise Institute , for example , argues that there should be no real international participation , either in the technology itself or in making rules that would regulate it . <p> " About 40% of the world 's population , mostly those in very poor countries , has not even heard of climate change ; therefore , insisting on proof of global informed consent as a precondition for testing climate engineering amounts to saying that climate engineering can never be tested . " <p> He continues : <p> " The US constitution enjoins our government to promote the general welfare , and the context is clearly a national one . A US government that allowed abstract notions of global informed consent to block action @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ out of office - and rightly so . " <p> Others argue that all the world 's peoples are absolutely entitled to have a say in issues that may affect them markedly . <p> Last year , a paper in Science contended that sun-obstructing technologies could increase drought . And if aerosol deployment in northern Asia , for example , affected the monsoon further south , should n't countries such as India , Bangladesh , Pakistan and Nepal have a say in whether the aerosols are deployed or not ? <p> " ... to ensure that ocean fertilisation activities do not take place until there is an adequate scientific basis on which to justify such activities , including assessing associated risks , and a global , transparent and effective control and regulatory mechanism is in place for these activities ; with the exception of small scale scientific research studies within coastal waters . " <p> This had practical ramifications for the German/Indian Lohafex expedition , which endured an on-off existence even as the Polarstern research ship was on its way to dump six tonnes of iron filings into the Southern Ocean @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> For the first time in an international treaty organisation , there is a concrete move58Kb PDF to go beyond iron fertilisation and apply international oversight across the field - including , presumably , anything that Dr Venter or his genome-engineering peers might come up with . <p> It tells governments to ensure ... <p> " ... that no climate-related geo-engineering activities take place until there is an adequate scientific basis on which to justify such activities and appropriate consideration of the associated risks for the environment and biodiversity and associated social , economic and cultural impacts . " <p> In the time-honoured way of UN matters , the paragraph is swaddled in square brackets , so is n't universally approved - Canada was reportedly leading opposition - but it 's likely to receive some kind of airing , if not resolution , in October . <p> Critics could certainly say it looks vague as it stands - does the word " activities " encompass research , for example , and if so , at what scales ? <p> You could also argue that the potential risks of geo-engineering affect much @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ it should be dealt with under this convention . <p> Activists , though , were delighted that the step had been taken , Neth Dano of the ETC Group commenting : <p> " Big industry and big science increasingly want to press ahead with geo-engineering either as a ' plan B ' or a free pass to avoid reducing emissions . <p> " It 's the big lie that lets them pretend that we can all carry on flying , driving , and consuming- business as usual ! " <p> So there it is : the gauntlet has been laid down to those who argue that research into and deployment of geo-engineering should be left to the world 's major powers and the companies that reside in those nations . <p> But if it 's accepted , would that mean research slows to a snail 's pace , risking the corollary that if and when we find the Earth needs a sunshield in space , we wo n't be ready to build one ? <h> Comments <h> About this blog <p> I 'm Richard Black , environment correspondent for the BBC @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ happening to our shared environment as the human population grows and our use of nature 's resources increases . <h> BBC links <p> This page is best viewed in an up-to-date web browser with style sheets ( CSS ) enabled . They talk about the same thing as we do : the weather . It 's the middle of May , and the owner of the domaine next door is having his roof done . Except that the work has stopped . " Demoiselles " , they call them . A friend claims to have found some meat , once . Everybody said it was the drink talking . <p> There is a marked tendency around here toward an eating philosophy that could best be summed up as , " If it moves , eat it " . The French are famed for their food , but their tastes can sometimes be less than epicurean . Some years ago they introduced a large South American rodent , the Coypu , into the ditches and little rivers , to exterminate rats . As happened with the rabbit and cane toad in Australia , in France the coypu became a bigger problem than the one it was supposed to exterminate . The big old rodent proliferated . The resourceful French had a ready solution : they put it on the menu . Tasty . <p> ... <p> If I can once more tread on the Editor 's good nature : Last year 's BBC Children in Need appeal raised a record-breaking ? @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Britain , despite desperate economic circumstances . @@1494877 <h> Miliband 's escape from The Holocaust - Exclusive <h> She was just 8 years old when her father was killed by the Nazis , but somehow w she survived and eventually found sanctuary in Britain . <p> Shares <h> Get politics news by email <p> Invalid e-mailThanks for subscribing ! Could not subscribe , try again later <p> On its own it 's a remarkable tale ... young Jewish girl manages to escape the Nazi death squads , hides in Poland for two years , then eventually escapes to Britain where she builds a new life . <p> What makes Dobra Jenta Kozak 's story even more remarkable is that she went on to have two sons , David and Ed . And today they are fighting for the leadership of the Labour Party . <p> Dobra - who changed her name to Marion to confuse the Nazis - was born in the Jewish quarter of Czestochowa , Southern Poland , in 1934 . As the daughter of a wealthy steel factory owner she enjoyed a prosperous family life . <p> But that was all torn apart @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Dobra went into hiding while 40,000 men , women and children from the city , including her father and grandparents , were rounded up and shot or sent to the gas chambers of Auschwitz and Treblinka . <p> Her school friends were killed in a cemetery and buried in mass graves where their bodies fell . But incredibly Marion , now aged 75 , survived after being taken in by people risking their own lives . <p> She has never spoken publicly about her escape . But this week the Sunday Mirror tracked down an orphan who shared those darkest of days in Czestochowa . <p> Sigmund Rolat , now 79 and a leading Jewish philanthropist , recalled the young Marion and the heroes , Jewish and non-Jewish , who risked their lives to help children escape the death camps . <p> He tells an amazing story of a German factory director called Litt , who rescued children from the Nazi soldiers by claiming he needed them to work for him . Mr Rolat says Litt was Czestochowa 's own Oskar Schindler , the German industrialist credited with saving 1,200 Jewish @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ film . <p> Sigmund was 12 when he was orphaned and his 18-year-old brother shot dead by Nazis . He told the Sunday Mirror : " I remember <p> Marion - she was known as Marisya Kozak then . She may have changed her name from Dobra to make it sound less Jewish to protect her from the Germans . <p> " Many brave people were fighting for their lives against the tyranny of the Nazis . Any idea that many Jews went to their deaths like sheep is not true . For the children , it was a daily battle to survive . " <p> Marion 's parents were David Miliband ( who David is named after ) and Bronislawa . Sigmund 's family lived near them and also owned a factory . <p> On September 3 , 1939 , the Germans stormed Czestochowa two days after taking Poland and seized the Kozak factory , turning it into a munitions centre . <p> Nazi soldiers burned down the synagogue on Christmas Day and in 1941 they forced Jewish families from their homes into a ghetto . On Yom Kippur in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , the Nazis set out to " liquidate " the ghetto - sending men , women and children to the gas chambers . Dawid Miliband took urgent steps to safeguard his family , making the heartbreaking decision to part from his wife Bronislawa , Marion and her sister Hadassa , who was four years younger . <p> Dawid stayed behind to look after his mother Adela and father Mauryc who were too frail to leave . Adela 's gravestone says she was killed by the Germans on July 18 , 1943 . It is thought her husband was also shot by Nazis . But Marion and her sister survived by being sent into hiding . <p> Sigmund said : " There were three ways a Jewish child could survive . The first was to hide . The second was to be placed in a convent if you were a girl , which is I think what happened to Marion , or be placed with a non-Jewish family willing to pass the child off as their own . " <p> Sigmund was 12 when the Germans came for him . But he @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ men and women were working in a factory before going to a slave labour camp and then on to the gas chambers . <p> Soon thousands of families from Czestochowa joined the three million Polish Jews who were sent to the gas chambers . In May 1943 , it was Sigmund 's turn to go to Treblinka . But he was saved by a German factory owner . Sigmund recalls : " They rounded up the children left in the ghetto - about 35 of us - and we were going to be sent to Treblinka when the German director - Litt - stepped in . He lied to the Nazi captain , saying he needed children to work in his factory because we had small hands . He saved us that day . " <p> It is not clear whether Marion was in the group of children spared alongside Sigmund but it is thought she was . At the age of 12 , Marion was sent to school in England by a Jewish backer . She won a place at university and met Ralph Miliband - David and Ed 's father - whose family were also Polish Jews . <p> Marion met Ralph , a university lecturer , while she was a student attending his classes at the London School of Economics . <p> They married in 1961 . <p> David was born in 1965 and Ed in 1969 . Ralph gained worldwide fame as a Marxist scholar , while historian Marion has written papers on women 's inter-war history . Ralph died in 1994 . <p> David Miliband met Sigmund last June when he visited Warsaw as Foreign Secretary . He paid tribute to those who protected his mother , saying : " My mother was born here , her life was saved by those who risked theirs sheltering her from Nazi oppression . @@1494946 <p> And yet , against all odds , America 's Dead Sea has become a source of wealth for many of the people who live along the lake 's southern and eastern shores . <h> Natural wealth <p> In Weber County , a single-track road links Interstate-15 to the shores of the lake , past grazing long-horn cattle and farms with old , canvas-covered prairie wagons or modern RVs - mobile homes - parked in their drives . <p> Image caption GSL Minerals has been operating here for four decades <p> Here , to the west of the city of Ogden and the Wasatch mountain range , sandwiched in between two of the lake 's many waterfowl sanctuaries , the low-rise industrial operations of Great Salt Lake Minerals Corporation lie tucked away at the side of a small hill , aptly known as Little Mountain . <p> GSL Minerals is essentially a mining operation that extracts salt , sulfate of potash , and magnesium-chloride from the lake 's brine . <p> The process is basic . The salty , mineral-rich lake water - or brine - is pumped @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ acres , leaving the sun to do much of the work . <p> " At various times of the evaporation process , each of those minerals crystalise and drop to the bottom , " explains GSL Minerals spokesman Dave Hyams . <h> Enhancing growth <p> The end result is satisfying for the people who work here as it turns out this lifeless lake has become a great source of growth elsewhere . <p> Potassium sulfate is very versatile . Most of it is used as organic specialty fertiliser to grow vegetables , fruits and nuts , though some is even used in the cosmetics and pharmaceuticals industry , or it is mixed in with ceramics or into plaster boards to speed up the drying process . <p> " And this is the only naturally occurring source of sulphate of potash in North America , " points out Mr Hyams . <p> The magnesium-chloride , meanwhile , is a nutrient used by wheat and corn growers , while some of the salt from the lake is used on roads to melt ice , as water softener , or as salt lick blocks @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ poured back into the lake , Mr Hyams explains , as there is no market for it . <h> More ponds <p> GSL Minerals ' activities have helped transform many of the local people 's view of the lake , which is now widely seen as a cash-cow providing jobs , tax earnings and profits . <p> The lake is at risk from population growth and climate change , and a persistent failure to see it as anything other than a source of industrial raw materials at best and a waste of water at worstProfessor Daniel Bedford , Weber State University <p> So as demand for its organic fertiliser from a growing agricultural sector continues to rise , GSL Minerals says it wants to build more evaporation ponds in the north-eastern corner of the lake . <p> " People are turning to healthier foods such as fruit and vegetables rather than wheat and corn , so demand for our potassium sulfate is set to at least double and perhaps even treble over the next four decades , " explains Mr Hyams . <p> GSL Minerals ' plan would result in the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ expansion would add approximately 91,000 acres of solar evaporation ponds , " according to a US Army Corps of Engineers study of the potential impact on the environment of GSL Minerals ' proposal , though Mr Hyams insists that figure includes some double counting and that the actual acreage would be 75,000 . <h> Environmental risks <p> GSL Minerals has operated in Great Salt Lake , alongside the lake 's birdlife , for four decades . Its operations are not in themselves polluting and the salt and minerals it extract are constantly replenished by the rivers that feed the lake . <p> So it is not surprising that the authorities here have largely welcomed the additional 70 or so jobs the expansion plan would add to its 330-strong workforce , along with the additional $10m per year it would contribute through property tax and mineral royalties . <p> But environmentalists are nevertheless concerned , insisting that the plans to expand the evaporation ponds pose a potential threat to a fragile ecosystem . <p> GSL Minerals ' expansion would result in more water being pumped from the lake , thus resulting in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the campaign group Friends of the Great Salt Lake . <p> Lower water means reduced shoreline , thus more crowding and a greater risk of disease and predator access for the bird population , according to Daniel Bedford , associate professor in the geography department at Weber State University . <p> Indeed , GSL Minerals ' activities could even result in the lake 's salt levels rising , " warns Professor Bedford observes in a paper published in the journal Environment . <p> In turn , this could result in a clash with " Utah 's multi-million dollar brine shrimp industry , which harvests brine shrimp eggs from the lake for sale to commercial aquaculture operations " , he says . <h> @@1495957 <h> The climate-change greenhouse in a datacentre <p> When you 're building a datacentre , the biggest problem you 've got is often getting rid of the heat generated by so many computers running in such a small area . Some data centres just pump it out into the outside world . Others use the excess energy to heat local homes . But TelecityGroup 's newest datacentre , Condorcet -- which opened in Paris earlier in the year , uses its heat to conduct research into climate change . <p> The building 's exterior is comprised of a massive arboretum - a greenhouse , which is maintained at the climatic conditions expected to be prevailing in France in 2050 . The French National Institute for Agricultural Research ( INRA ) operates a research centre there , growing plants from around the world to investigate which will be viable to grow when climate change 's effects are starting to be felt in the country . <p> Unfortunately , the facility is n't open to the public due to security concerns -- only researchers for INRA and TelecityGroup employees are @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the interior of the facility looks like , then you can browse Wired 's photo gallery here . <p> To add to the data centre 's green credentials , the company claims that it 's the most efficient in Europe . Efficiency is judged by measuring the power coming in and dividing that figure by the amount of power used to run the computer equipment that it contains . A totally efficient datacentre would have an efficiency of 1 , meaning that no power is wasted , and Condorcet 's figure is 1.3 , when running at optimum load . <p> " That 's equivalent " , Michael Tobin , CEO of TelecityGroup told Wired , " to 2,500 metric tons of CO2 per year when compared to the consumption of a legacy data centre of the same capacity . Our aim was to build Europe 's most energy efficient data centre . It is virtually impossible to achieve ( an efficiency rating ) lower than this with the technology available today . "

The environment featured in the news as usual -- there was a report saying the UK could power itself six times over with offshore renewable energy , and activists scaled London 's BP building in protest at the oil disaster in the Gulf of Mexico . Both issues are pertinent to the greater cause , but the real sign of progress took place at the Houses of Parliament , where Caroline Lucas , the leader of the Green Party , was sworn in as Britain 's first Green Member of Parliament . <p> When we meet at Westminster she is awaiting the call to be officially anointed an MP , a process that requires , after much hanging around , declaring loyalty to our head of state , the Queen . The people of Brighton Pavilion have elected me to do a job , and that 's what I have to do to do it . " <p> As for the hanging around , what 's a few hours given that Lucas , in terms of her employment with the Green Party , has been hanging around for 24 years for this moment ? The party itself has been waiting almost 40 years for a sniff of power . <p> Did she imagine it would take this long to get a single MP into the Commons ? She laughs . " Absolutely not . I joined in 1986 . There was a new awareness of the seriousness of the climate crisis but also of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a fair and effective way . And then we had our wonderful result in 1989 , winning 15 per cent of the vote in the European elections . " <p> In terms of " bursts of enthusiasm " , she says she is certain the current media interest in her will wane with time . She is , though , accompanied by an independent film-maker who is recording her first year in office . She brushes off his presence modestly , saying it might well come to nothing . But as Lucas , her party , constituents , supporters anywhere in the country and indeed across Europe and the world know , it has to come to something . Not necessarily the bust ups , back stabbing , bitching and in-fighting that makes good telly , but something that genuinely furthers the possibilities of green power in government and clears the way for more Green MPs . The Greens want emissions reduced by 10 per cent year on year . It 's unlikely they 'll get their wish on this and other policies . What Lucas can do is make sure that by the end of this Parliament , however long the coalition lasts , her party appears yet more electable -- and that any last remnant of the Greens as a marginal group of mung bean idealist activists is erased forever . <p> " It 's a huge responsibility , " she says . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ But it 's a huge honour as well , and while I 'm on my own in here , I feel very grounded by the support I have from my party and NGOs outside . I hope it 's not impossible to demonstrate you can do politics without selling your principles . " <p> Lucas wants " environmental justice for future generations , " she says . But has n't our new Prime Minister , David Cameron , just made the very same commitment , promising his Government will be the " greenest ever " ? <p> " That 's not very difficult , is it ? " she says . " Labour has been so appalling on the environment . " I 'm desperately disappointed that the zeal for opening up this place so people 's voices really count , by introducing some form of proportional representation , has been lost , " says Lucas . <p> PR would have given the Green Party a much better chance of winning more seats in future elections . Despite decades of study , nobody has managed to translate any cetacean vocalisations into human language , or to prove that their songs have much greater information content than those of birds . The largest whales , for one thing , have a grazing lifestyle - feeding on fish or krill as cattle feed on grass - which gives little evolutionary pressure for the development of advanced minds . Evolution does not build in excess . Toothed whales , especially dolphins , have a more intellectually demanding way of life , as did our primitive ancestors . @@1497284 <p> Climate change will trigger a dramatic and sudden decline in the number of polar bears , a new study has concluded . <p> The research is the first to directly model how changing climate will affect polar bear reproduction and survival . <p> Based on what is known of polar bear physiology , behaviour and ecology , it predicts pregnancy rates will fall and fewer bears will survive fasting during longer ice-free seasons . <p> These changes will happen suddenly as bears pass a ' tipping point ' . <p> Details of the research are published in the journal Biological Conservation . <p> Educated guesses <p> Until now , most studies measuring polar bear survival have relied on a method called " mark and recapture " . <p> We may not see any substantial effect on polar bear reproduction and survival until some threshold is passed . <p> At that point reproduction and survival will decline dramatically and very rapidly <p> Peter MolnarUniversity of Alberta , Edmonton , Canada <p> This involves repeatedly catching polar bears in a population over several years , which is cost and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ have gathered on polar bear populations varies greatly : for example , datasets span up to four decades in the best studied populations in Western Hudson Bay and Southern Beaufort Sea , but are almost non-existent for bears in some parts of Russia . <p> Even more difficult is measuring how survival and reproduction might change under future climatic conditions . <p> " Some populations are expected to go extinct with climate warming , while others are expected to persist , albeit at a reduced population size , " says Dr Peter Molnar of the University of Alberta , Edmonton , Canada . <p> However , these projections are essentially educated guesses , based on experts judging or extrapolating how current population trends might continue as the climate changes . <p> " So we 've looked at the underlying mechanisms of polar bear ecology to assist our understanding of what will happen in a warming world , " Dr Molnar told the BBC . <p> Fasting and mating <p> Dr Molnar , Professor Andrew Derocher and colleagues from the University of Alberta and York University , Toronto focused on the physiology @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ these might change as temperatures increase . <p> " We developed a model for the mating ecology of polar bears . The model estimates how many females in a population will be able to find a mate during the mating season , and thus get impregnated . " <p> " In both cases , the expected changes in reproduction and survival were non-linear , " explains Dr Molnar . <p> " That is , as the climate warms , we may not see any substantial effect on polar bear reproduction and survival for a while , up until some threshold is passed , at which point reproduction and survival will decline dramatically and very rapidly . " <p> The US Endangered Species Act lists the polar bear as " Threatened " . <p> The latest US assessment of the conservation status of polar bears included the only two previous studies to assess the impact of climate change , but these extrapolated population trends , rather than directly modelling how the ecology of polar bears may alter . <p> The new study by Dr Molnar 's team offers a way to improve @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ declines than those found by the US assessment . <p> " Canada has about two-thirds of the world 's polar bears , but their conservation assessment of polar bears did n't take climate change seriously , " says Dr Molnar , a flaw noted by the IUCN/SSC Polar Bear Specialist Group last year . <p> " Our view is that the Canadian assessment should be redone , properly accounting for climate change effects . <p> " The status of polar bears is likely much more dire than suggested by the Canadian report , " he adds . <p> " For instance , for a while we will only see small changes in summer fasting season survival in Western Hudson Bay . <p> " But eventually mortality will dramatically increase when a certain threshold is passed ; for example , while starvation mortality is currently negligible , up to one-half of the male population would starve if the fasting season in Western Hudson Bay was extended from currently four to about six months . " <p> This page is best viewed in an up-to-date web browser with style sheets ( CSS ) @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ content of this page in your current browser , you will not be able to get the full visual experience . Please consider upgrading your browser software or enabling style sheets ( CSS ) if you are able to do so .

@@1498728 <h> Clive Hamilton : ' solving climate change is out of the question ' <h> Author Clive Hamilton on why we 've left it too late to stop climate change , his horror over geoengineering and the urgent need to become citizens rather than consumers <p> Tom Levitt : What does this book say to the environmental movement ? Clive Hamilton : One should not assume that environmental activists are necessarily in the vanguard of their understanding of the science and its implications . I think a lot of environmentalists are in denial about the severity of the crisis that is starting to unfold . <p> The science does demand a wholesale re-thinking of how we go about dealing with climate change , because if , as the science shows overwhelmingly , there is no possibility of limiting warming to 2 degrees and we 'll be lucky if we can limit it to 4 degrees , we 're gon na have a radically transformed climate on earth and this changes everything . <p> Every other book says ' yes , it 's serious but it 's not too @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ have much time and it 's urgent and we must act immediately ' but they always say ' it 's not too late ' . Either because the authors believe that or they feel if they say it 's too late then people will lose hope and become immobilised . <p> I can understand that concern , but the truth is we are past the point of no return , so we have to completely re-think the problem and not pretend there are things we can do that will save the situation . <p> TL : Do you worry that if we accept the reality of climate change we will bring out the worst in human instincts ? <p> CH : There is a serious danger of that - military forces in the major countries are planning for different types of conflict to merge in decades to come so they are viewing climate change as what they call a ' threat intensifier ' . ? ? <p> Progressive voices in the environmental movement need to start thinking about how we can shift the political system so that the response to climate @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ be , and that is why I talk about the need to democratise survivability as much as we can and stop the poor and powerless losing out to the rich and well-connected . <p> TL : You quote a 13-year girl in your book saying ' we should accept the reality of climate change and deal with it ' . So what is the next step ? <p> CH : The purpose of this book is to confront people with reality of climate change rather than the comfortable stories that it could still be okay . When you really look at what the scientists are saying it causes us to re-think everything , including the whole notion of adaptation because previously it was thought of as a marginal activity . <p> If the pattern of climate change unfolds as broadly expected it will be a process of constant adaptation and there is a danger of making investments that will become dated in decades when we realise we did n't go far enough . <p> I worry about immediately leaping to what we can do because I think we have to accept @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ we are anywhere near doing that yet . ? <p> TL : Is there a danger that this kind of reality will lead to apathy ? <p> CH : The only way we can maintain our integrity and dignity is not to retreat into apathy and indifference but to become more active and that includes civil disobedience if necessary . ? ? Telling the truth does n't mean you stop acting if the truth is very bad . This is not a justification for helplessness based on despair . But if you 're not despairing your not listening to the scientists because that is the reality . <p> It 's too late to avoid dramatic climate change this century , but there are degrees of changes that can be influenced by what we do over , in particular , the next decade or two . ? ? <p> You can sit back and say , oh we 're going have terrible climate change and there 's nothing I can do about that , but if those people have children then they are pretty poor parents not to worry about the conditions @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is a pretty powerful incentive to as much as we can to at least delay the worst effects and begin preparing ourselves for life in a transformed climate . ? ? <p> TL : Can we solve climate change ? <p> CH : We ca n't avoid it because we 've left it too late . Ten years ago if the major countries of the world had embarked on far reaching policies to reduce their GHG emissions we would have had a good chance of avoiding most of the effects . ? ? <p> All the science suggests that in this century and the next the climate is going to be changed in many ways hostile to humans and life in general - we 're not going to avoid that . <p> The question of solving it is out of the question . It 's not like urban air pollution , where when it gets bad enough you introduce some laws on catalytic converters and it goes away , because we have set off a huge process of change in the global climate system which has its own dynamic . ? @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ are saying that we humans should take control of the atmosphere of the earth in order to modify it in ways that suit us , in a way to operate a global thermostat , which we then fiddle with in a way that is in our interests - assuming that the great diversity of interests around the world could meet some consensus , which they probably could n't . <p> It 's rich white blokes saying , ' well we 've got to take control of this problem and we 're going to engineer the atmosphere ' . I just find that the most audacious and terrifying proposal that humans have ever put forward . <p> Fundamentally , the technological capabilities of human beings are far in advance of the psychological maturity , which is why I think if we embark on geoengineering built on the psychological predisposition and weakness that have given us climate change we are only going to make the problem worse . I think we are insufficiently evolved as a species to responsibly intervene in the earth 's atmosphere . <p> TL : You talk about the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to consume is a block on tackling climate change ? <p> CH : It 's a huge obstacle because people have become habituated to their consumerist lifestyle . I mean ten years ago people were quite happy without mobile phones but if you took them away today there would be riots in the streets . But only for a little while , because people would become quite used it . <p> People are afraid of change and use consumerism in our types of affluent societies as a way of defining themselves and gaining a sense of personal identity . It worries me when environment groups pander to that by effectively arguing we can consume our way out of trouble and I do n't think we can . <p> However , I do n't think consumerism is essential to human nature . Most cultures in most times have n't been driven by astounding greed and materialism . Forty years ago in the UK the levels of consumerism and materialism were vastly less than today so we 're talking about a very modern phenomenon . <p> Not to say human greed has n't @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ only in very recent times that greed and intense materialism has become the dominant characteristic of certain society . ? But even in our types of affluent societies there are certain sub cultures that do n't engage in that intensely materialist treadmill that so dominates public thinking and policy and private behavior , for example downshifters . <p> People are able to escape the clutches of consumerism but it takes a wrench , and I do n't think we have time to wait for a possible social revolution in which we transcend consumerism and materialism before we take measures to tackle climate change . <p> TL : So what can individuals do or are they powerless ? <p> CH : They need to think of themselves as citizens not consumers . @@1498881 <h> Extreme droughts to be ' more common ' <p> Britain is heading for water shortages and crop failures as extreme droughts like that of 1976 become more frequent , experts have warned . <p> A Met Office study on how climate change could affect the frequency of extreme droughts in the UK has found they will become more common by 2100 , and to put the droughts in context , conditions seen in 1976 were used as a benchmark -- one of the worst droughts on record . <p> The Met Office climate model was used to run a number of simulations and in the worst case scenarios , extreme droughts could happen once every decade -- making them about 10 times more frequent than today . <p> Eleanor Burke , climate extremes scientist with the Met Office , said understanding how droughts will affect the UK in the future is vital for plans to adapt to climate change . <p> She said : " Severe droughts such as the one seen in 1976 have a big impact -- causing water shortages , health risks , fire @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ frequency of these events will change is therefore very important to planning for the future . " <p> Further research will be aimed at assessing how likely each of the climate model 's results is , to give better guidance for people to plan for the consequences of climate change . <p> While it culminated in the summer of 1976 , the drought was actually an 18-month period of below average rainfall starting in May 1975 . Only half the normal rainfall fell between June and August in 1976 . <p> Temperatures were 4C above average between June and August across much of southern England , and the bone dry conditions proved a major hazard , with fires breaking out daily . @@1500135 <h> Why every decade could now have a baking-hot summer like 1976 ( or so says the Met Office ) <p> Their promises of a barbecue summer and a mild winter in 2009 could hardly have been more wrong , but the Met Office is now predicting extreme droughts like the one that gripped Britain in the summer of 1976 could become much more common . <p> The drought of 1976 culminated in a 18-month period of below average rainfall which started in May 1975 . The period was marked by daily fires and dry river beds , while agriculture suffered badly , with an estimated ? 500 million in failed crops . <p> A study by the meteorologists looked at how frequently droughts could occur in the UK by 2100 in the face of global warming . <p> Cracking up : The bone-dry Walton-on-Thames Reservoir in 1976 <p> High and dry : A picture of the River Thames in 1976 taken from Kew Bridge looking in the direction of Brentford <h> THE DROUGHT OF 1976 <p> While it culminated in the summer of 1976 , the drought @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in May 1975 <p> Only half the normal rainfall fell between June and August in 1976 <p> Temperatures were 4C above average between June and August across much of southern England <p> The bone dry conditions caused a major hazard , with fires breaking out on a daily basis - in Surrey , the fire brigade answered 11,000 fire related calls in 5 months <p> Agriculture suffered badly , with an estimated ? 500 million in failed crops <p> Dry ground saw a surge in subsidence claims on property , with costs totalling about ? 60m <p> Water became scarce - a Drought Act was passed and there was widespread water rationing <p> Some rivers almost completely dried up , such as the Don and Sheaf in Sheffield <p> The researchers ran a series of simulations of their climate model to see how weather patterns may change in the future , and the majority showed extreme dry spells would become more common . <p> There was a range of 11 different versions of the model . <p> At the lower end of the spectrum 1976-style droughts - where temperatures were 40C @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ England - remained as rare as they are today , occurring only once every 50 to 100 years . <p> However , most of the outcomes showed droughts becoming more frequent . Some simulations suggested they could even occur once every decade . This would make droughts 10 times more common than they currently are . <p> The researchers , who based their study on the mid-range of projections for how emissions and temperatures might rise , did not assume any outcome of the model was more likely than another . <p> Future research will be aiming to assess how likely each result is , to give better guidance for people to plan for the consequences of climate change . <p> Eleanor Burke , climate extremes scientist with the Met Office , said understanding how frequently droughts will occur was important for plans to adapt to global warming . <p> ' Severe droughts such as the one seen in 1976 have a big impact - causing water shortages , health risks , fire hazards , crop failure and subsidence . <p> ' Understanding how the frequency of these events will change @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ' <p> 2010 : Spectators enjoy the hot weather during a golf championship in Surrey . @@1500250 <h> A historic moment for anyone who cares about the environment <p> History does n't always come in thunderclaps or cheering crowds , and yesterday it was made with very little outward fuss when a woman in a pale blue trouser suit got to her feet from a green leather bench and began to speak . <p> It was precisely 3.30 in the afternoon , and the Deputy Speaker of the House of Commons , Hugh Bayley , had just issued a two-word invitation : " Caroline Lucas . " And with that , the first MP of the Green Party , in fact the first MP of a new national party for many years , began her maiden speech and her party 's political life at Westminster . Henceforth , the environment has its own representation in our politics . <p> It had been a long journey to get there , she said . Caroline Lucas has led the way back to reality , and to the realisation of the truly noble aim of having a politics based on concern for the Earth , as much as on concern for equality , or freedom -- the ideal of Petra Kelly of Germany 's Die Gr ? nen , the charismatic inspiration for Green parties the world over . <p> I have followed her long journey closely over those two subsequent decades @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ .
